**Step 1: Analyze the spatial contradiction**

(Testimony 8). However, May's testimony states, "The victim dodged an attack then ran to the right, but she was caught and struck." (Testimony 9). These statements directly contradict each other regarding the direction Mia Fey ran.

**Step 2: Check if any spatial propositions apply**

Prop 5: "If Mia Fey ran to the left and White gave chase then White is behind Mia Fey." This proposition is relevant.

**Step 3: If applicable propositions exist, state which proposition(s) and explain how they reveal the contradiction**

Prop 5 directly relates to the contradiction. White's testimony (8) implies that if Mia ran left and he chased, he would be behind her. May's testimony (9) states Mia ran *right*. If Mia ran right, White would not be behind her as described in Prop 5. The conflicting directions of Mia's run, as described by White and May, create a spatial inconsistency.

LLM OUTPUT (RAW RESPONSE):
------------------------------
**Step 1: Analyze the spatial contradiction**

The contradiction lies between Testimony 0 ("Indeed, it's true that I looked away from the door for a brief moment.") and the general implication of Atmey's account of being attacked by Mask☆DeMasque. If Atmey looked away from the door, he wouldn't have been able to see Mask☆DeMasque attack him. Atmey describes a direct confrontation and being struck from behind ("Bam! ...And that's all she wrote." and "His first blow struck true!"). Looking away implies a lack of awareness and a position that wouldn't allow for such a direct attack.

**Step 2: Check if any spatial propositions apply**

Prop 1: Atmey can potentially see Mask☆DeMasque - This proposition is directly relevant. Atmey's testimony implies he *did* see Mask☆DeMasque, but his admission of looking away contradicts this possibility.

**Step 3: If applicable propositions exist, state which and explain**

Prop 1 states that Atmey *can potentially* see Mask☆DeMasque. Atmey's testimony about looking away directly contradicts this potential. If he looked away, he couldn't have seen the attack, making his description of the confrontation inconsistent with the possibility of observation.

**Step 4: If none of the listed propositions apply, explain the spatial reasoning**

N/A - Prop 1 directly applies.

**Step 2: Check if any spatial propositions apply**

Prop 2: The storeroom is inside the Bigg Building.
Prop 3: The Committee chamber is in the same room as the storeroom.
Prop 4: The secret elevator is inside the Bigg Building.

These propositions are directly relevant to the spatial contradiction.

**Step 3: If applicable propositions exist, explicitly state which proposition(s) and explain how they reveal the contradiction**

Props 2, 3, and 4 reveal the contradiction. Prop 2 and 3 establish the spatial relationship between the storeroom and Committee chamber, both being within the Bigg Building. Prop 4 introduces the secret elevator, also within the Bigg Building. Testimony 3 claims the *only* exit from the storeroom is the hatch to the viewing platform. However, the existence of the secret elevator (Prop 4) provides an alternative exit route, directly contradicting the exclusivity claimed in Testimony 3.

**Step 4: If none of the listed propositions apply, explain the spatial reasoning used to identify the contradiction**

Not applicable, as the listed propositions directly address the spatial relationships involved.

This creates a contradiction because the camera's purpose and Lotta's actions don't align. **Step 2: Check if any spatial propositions apply**

**Step 3: If applicable propositions exist, state which proposition(s) and explain how they reveal the contradiction**

Prop 1 applies. The camera facing the lake (Prop 1) suggests a passive observation method. Lotta's use of binoculars (Testimony 4) suggests an active observation method. The contradiction arises because the camera was set up to automatically record, while Lotta actively used binoculars to look. This implies a disconnect between her stated purpose (meteor photography via camera) and her actual actions (active observation with binoculars).

**Step 4: If none of the listed propositions apply, explain the spatial reasoning used to identify the contradiction**

The spatial reasoning focuses on the intended function of the camera versus Lotta's actions. The camera's orientation (facing the lake) suggests a passive, automated observation. Lotta's use of binoculars implies a deliberate, active observation. These two actions are spatially inconsistent with each other, creating the contradiction.

The contradiction lies between Acro's testimony about his physical limitations and the implication that he could have known the location of the Ringmaster's head. Testimony 2 states he could not have exerted the force to lift the bust and look out the window simultaneously. This implies he couldn't have positioned the bust to strike the Ringmaster's head, as knowing the head's location would require looking out the window.

**Step 2: Check if any spatial propositions apply**

Prop 3 is relevant. It states that if Acro is on the third floor of the lodging house and the Ringmaster's room is northeast of the circus, Acro *could potentially* see the Ringmaster's room. Acro's testimony directly contradicts this potential.

**Step 3: If applicable propositions exist, state which proposition(s) and explain how they reveal the contradiction**

Prop 3 establishes a *potential* line of sight. Acro's testimony (Testimony 2) explicitly denies that he could have looked out the window. This directly contradicts the potential line of sight described in Prop 3. If Acro couldn't look out the window, he couldn't have seen the Ringmaster's room, regardless of their relative positions.

**Step 4: If none of the listed propositions apply, explain the spatial reasoning used to identify the contradiction**

The spatial reasoning is based on the concept of visibility and physical limitations. Acro claims he couldn't lift the bust *and* look out the window. Knowing the location of the victim's head would require looking out the window. Therefore, his inability to look out the window prevents him from knowing the head's location, contradicting the possibility of him dropping the bust on the victim's head.

------------------------------
**Step 1: Analyze the spatial contradiction**

The contradiction lies between Evidence 6 (Newspaper Clipping 2) and Testimonies 2, 3, 4, and 5 given by Ini Miney. Evidence 6 states that Mimi Miney died in a car accident on May 24th, falling asleep at the wheel. Ini's testimonies describe Mimi's car as a "really shiny, bright red sports car," a "totally special model from England," and Mimi being very protective of it, refusing to let "newbs" drive it. Ini also states she was in the passenger seat that night. This creates a contradiction because if Mimi was so protective of her car and wouldn't let anyone else drive it, how could Ini have been in the passenger seat? Furthermore, the newspaper clipping explicitly states Mimi was driving when the accident occurred.

**Step 2: Check if any spatial propositions apply**

Prop 1: Mimi Miney cannot be at a different location at time ZZZ if Mimi Miney is at location highway center divide at time May 24.
Prop 2: Ini Miney cannot be at a different location at time May 24 if Ini Miney is at location highway center divide at time May 24.

**Step 3: If applicable propositions exist, state which proposition(s) and explain how they reveal the contradiction**

Prop 1 and Prop 2 are relevant. Prop 1 states Mimi cannot be elsewhere if she was at the highway center divide at May 24. Prop 2 states Ini cannot be elsewhere if she was at the highway center divide at May 24. Ini's testimonies imply she was *with* Mimi in the car, at the highway center divide. However, the newspaper clipping states Mimi was the driver, implying she was solely responsible for the car's location and actions. Ini's statements about being in the passenger seat contradict the newspaper's account of Mimi being the driver and therefore solely responsible for the accident.

**Step 4: If none of the listed propositions apply, explain the spatial reasoning used to identify the contradiction**

The spatial reasoning focuses on the implied location of Ini and Mimi at the time of the accident. Ini's testimonies place her in the car *with* Mimi, sharing the same spatial location. The newspaper clipping, however, isolates Mimi as the driver, implying she was the sole occupant of the car at the time of the accident. This creates a spatial contradiction regarding who was present and where they were located during the car crash.

------------------------------
**Step 1: Analyze the spatial contradiction**

Testimony 3 states: "And then... like, I took my sister to the Channeling Chamber." Testimony 4 states: "I, like, didn't see anyone on the way to, like, the Channeling Chamber." Evidence 1 (Guidemap) states that the Side Room is connected to the Channeling Chamber via a winding path through the hallway. Ini's testimony implies she walked with her sister (Pearl) from somewhere (presumably the Side Room, as mentioned in Testimony 0) to the Channeling Chamber. If she walked with Pearl, she *would* have seen Pearl. The contradiction lies in Ini claiming to not have seen anyone while simultaneously stating she escorted her sister to the Channeling Chamber.

**Step 2: Check if any spatial propositions apply**

Prop 1: Ini is in the same room as Pearl Fey. This is relevant because Ini's testimony implies she was with Pearl.
Prop 3: Maya Fey is to the right of the HHH if HHH is inside the Channeling Chamber. This is not relevant to the contradiction.

Prop 5: Maya Fey can see Mimi Miney if Mimi Miney is in front of Maya Fey. This is not relevant.

**Step 3: If applicable propositions exist, state which proposition(s) and explain how they reveal the contradiction**

Prop 1 (Ini is in the same room as Pearl Fey) is key. Ini's testimony states she *took* her sister to the Channeling Chamber. "Taking" someone implies being in close proximity, and therefore, being in the same room. If she was in the same room as Pearl, she *would* have seen her. Her subsequent claim of not seeing anyone contradicts the implication of her actions.

**Step 4: If none of the listed propositions apply, explain the spatial reasoning used to identify the contradiction**

The spatial reasoning is based on the understanding of "taking" someone somewhere. It implies a shared space and visibility. Ini's actions (taking her sister) contradict her statement (not seeing anyone) within the context of the spatial relationship between the Side Room and the Channeling Chamber as described in Evidence 1.

None of the spatial propositions directly address the temporal contradiction.

**Step 3: N/A - No applicable propositions**

**Step 4: Spatial reasoning**

The contradiction arises from a conflict in the timeline of events. The autopsy report (Evidence 1) and Crime Photo 2 (Evidence 3) establish a precise time of death (3:05). Phoenix's testimony (Testimony 3) describes an event (falling on the umbrella and a loud noise) that, according to his account, occurred *after* this time. This creates a logical impossibility: a person cannot perform an action after they are already dead. The spatial element is the victim's position and actions *relative to* the time of death.

**The answer JSON should strictly be the last line, with nothing before or after it.**

LLM OUTPUT (RAW RESPONSE):
------------------------------
**Step 1: Analyze the spatial contradiction**

Testimony 1 states, "When I opened the door to the joint, I saw one ugly scene." Testimony 2 states, "The guy was laid out over the table, stiff as concrete." Testimony 3 states, "I figured if the place wasn't hot already, it was gonna be, so I split." This sequence implies Tigre entered the restaurant, immediately saw the victim, and then left quickly. However, Evidence 12 (Crime Photo) describes a scene with a coffee cup knocked over, stained tablecloth, and scattered papers near the victim. This suggests a degree of disarray beyond simply finding a body. The photo implies an event *happened* at the table, not just the discovery of a corpse. Tigre's quick exit doesn't account for the scene described in the photo.

**Step 2: Check if any spatial propositions** from the list above apply to this contradiction

Prop 1: The victim is behind the HHH if HHH is covered by ZZZ and ZZZ is behind the YYY and YYY is covered by Tres Bien Floor Plans. - Not relevant.
Prop 2: Tigre is far from the ZZZ if Tigre is covered by Tres Bien and Tres Bien is far from the ZZZ - Not relevant.
Prop 3: The coffee cup is to the right of the ZZZ if the coffee cup is covered by Tres Bien and Tres Bien is to the right of the ZZZ - Not relevant.
Prop 4: Tigre held the Scooter with the left hand if left-hand fingerprints of Tigre are on the Scooter - Not relevant.
Prop 5: The victim falls backward if the victim is hit from the front - Potentially relevant, but doesn't directly address the contradiction.

**Step 3: If applicable propositions exist,** explicitly state which proposition(s) and explain how they reveal the contradiction

Prop 5 is tangentially relevant. If the victim fell backward, it implies he was hit from the front. Tigre's testimony doesn't mention any interaction or struggle, only the discovery of the body. The Crime Photo suggests a more chaotic scene than Tigre's account implies.

**Step 4: If none of the listed propositions apply,** explain the spatial reasoning used to identify the contradiction

The contradiction lies in the implied spatial narrative. Tigre's testimony creates a spatial sequence: entry -> observation of the body -> immediate departure. The Crime Photo presents a different spatial narrative: a scene of disarray (overturned coffee cup, scattered papers) *around* the body, suggesting an event occurred *before* the body was discovered. Tigre's quick exit doesn't align with the evidence of a disrupted scene.

None of the listed spatial propositions directly reveal the contradiction.

The testimonies establish that Gavèlle used the elevator, while the evidence shows the president did not. This discrepancy suggests the testimony is inaccurate regarding the direction of the bullet's impact.

Olga's testimony doesn't account for this pre-existing state, suggesting a misrepresentation of the sequence of events.

**Step 4: If none of the listed propositions apply, explain the spatial reasoning used to identify the contradiction**

The spatial reasoning is based on the implied sequence of events. The photo depicts a static scene, a "snapshot" of the aftermath. Olga's testimony describes a dynamic action (grabbing the bottle) that, according to her account, should have preceded the victim's slumped position. The photo's spatial arrangement contradicts the implied temporal order in Olga's testimony.

This inconsistency highlights the spatial contradiction between Olga's testimony and the crime scene depiction.

**Step 4: If none of the listed propositions apply, explain the spatial reasoning used to identify the contradiction**

The spatial reasoning involves understanding the physical implications of a blow to the forehead versus a back-leaning posture. A blow to the forehead requires a forward trajectory. A back-leaning posture makes a forward blow less likely and suggests a blow to the back of the head. The contradiction arises from the incompatibility of these two spatial elements.

**Step 2:** **Check if any spatial propositions** from the list above apply to this contradiction

**Step 3:** **If applicable propositions exist,** explicitly state which proposition(s) and explain how they reveal the contradiction

**Step 4:** **If none of the listed propositions apply,** explain the spatial reasoning used to identify the contradiction

**Your answer must end with a JSON format like so:** {"evidence": 2, "testimony": 3}

**The answer JSON should strictly be the last line, with nothing before or after it.**

LLM OUTPUT (RAW RESPONSE):
------------------------------
**Step 1: Analyze the spatial contradiction**

The contradiction lies between Testimony 1 ("That's when I saw Juniper dragging a large mat from behind the stage.") and Testimony 3 ("She was heading towards me, so I assume she was on her way to the maintenance area."). O'Conner states Juniper was dragging a mat *from behind the stage* and then assumes she was heading *towards him* (the vantage point near the maintenance area). This implies a spatial relationship: Juniper started behind the stage and was moving towards the maintenance area. However, the act of dragging a large mat significantly impacts movement. It's difficult to move quickly and efficiently while dragging a large mat, especially over a distance. The testimony doesn't account for the time and effort required to drag the mat, and the assumption of a direct path to O'Conner seems unlikely given the impediment.

**Step 2: Check if any spatial propositions apply**

Prop 2: "If Juniper was heading towards O'Conner then Juniper was on her way to the vantage point near the maintenance area." This proposition directly relates to the contradiction.

**Step 3: If applicable propositions exist, explicitly state which proposition(s) and explain how they reveal the contradiction**

Prop 2 states that if Juniper was heading towards O'Conner, she was on her way to the vantage point near the maintenance area. Testimony 3 *asserts* this. However, Testimony 1 states she was dragging a large mat from behind the stage. The act of dragging a mat creates a significant spatial constraint. It's improbable that someone dragging a large mat could quickly and directly head towards a distant vantage point. The proposition assumes a relatively unimpeded movement, which is contradicted by the action described in Testimony 1. The testimony implies a quick movement, while the action implies a slow and cumbersome one.

**Step 4: If none of the listed propositions apply, explain the spatial reasoning used to identify the contradiction**

N/A - Prop 2 directly applies.

Evidence 5 introduces an obstruction (closed shutters) that should have blocked that line of sight. Testimony 3 claims the line of sight was not blocked. This creates a spatial contradiction: the testimony implies an unobstructed view, while the evidence implies an obstructed view.

If he was fleeing to the right (hallway with the phone), and the demon emerged *after* he fled, then the demon must have emerged from the direction of the phone.

**Step 3: If applicable propositions exist, explicitly state which proposition(s) and explain how they reveal the contradiction**

None of the provided spatial propositions directly illuminate the contradiction. The contradiction is based on the relative positions and timing of events described in the testimonies and the crime scene diagram.

**Step 4: If none of the listed propositions apply, explain the spatial reasoning used to identify the contradiction**

The spatial reasoning involves understanding the layout of the crime scene (Evidence 5) and the sequence of events as described by L'Belle. Testimony 4 places L'Belle fleeing to the right, towards the phone. Testimony 3 places the demon's emergence *after* this flight. This creates a spatial conflict: if L'Belle fled to the right, and the demon emerged afterward, the demon must have emerged from the direction of the phone, which contradicts the implied location of the demon's prison.

**Step 4: If none of the listed propositions apply, explain the spatial reasoning used to identify the contradiction**

The contradiction arises from the conflicting spatial descriptions. Testimony 3 places Dr. Buff facing the bookshelves, implying he's positioned *away* from an attacker coming from behind. Testimony 4 and 5 describe Datz attacking from *behind*. These two descriptions are spatially incompatible.

**Step 4: If none of the listed propositions apply, explain the spatial reasoning used to identify the contradiction**

None of the spatial propositions directly reveal the contradiction.

Her inability to see Yatagarasu suggests she wasn't actually in the next room over, or that the room was arranged in a way that blocked her view despite being adjacent.
